People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

It’s made things tense with the other staff, and a few customers too.\n\nAt church, I’ve kind of stepped back from helping lead planning things because people were getting frustrated with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you're trying to organize your work or tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. When I try to sort everything, it just feels like too much. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F98.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess... I’m here because I can’t seem to keep things in order at work anymore. Tickets, receipts, notes, customer jobs—they all end up piled together, and then I don’t know what to handle first.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on a single task at work?\n\nPatient: Yes. I can start checking the day’s dry-cleaning orders, but after a few minutes my mind just… goes somewhere else. Labels, choir things, stuff at home. Then I look back and realize I missed a lot of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nDoctor: Are you having problems with your relationships at work or at home because of these issues?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly at work. Orders have gone out late, and I’ve mixed up billing more than once. It’s made things tense with the other staff, and a few customers too.\n\nAt church, I’ve kind of stepped back from helping lead planning things because people were getting frustrated with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you're trying to organize your work or tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. When I try to sort everything, it just feels like too much. The tickets, receipts, notes, customer jobs… they all end up in one pile, and then I can’t tell what I’m supposed to do first.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble organizing and focusing, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: It’s not really new. I mean… even back in grade school, teachers would tell my mother I was bright, but scattered. I’d talk out of turn, lose papers, and need reminders to finish my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, like you can't sit still?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess so. In meetings, like at church or those fabric-care trainings, I’ll keep finding reasons to get up—water, papers, checking on something. Everybody else is sitting, and I just… don’t stay put very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do that sometimes. I’ll jump in before my daughter or a customer is even done asking, or during a training, and then I realize I answered the wrong thing. It’s embarrassing, so I try not to, but… it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble controlling your emotions when things don't go as planned?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… I get irritated sometimes when plans change, like anyone, but I don’t know that I’d call it trouble controlling my emotions. I usually just try to keep it to myself.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often described as physically overactive, such as running, climbing, or having difficulty playing quietly?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I don’t remember being told that much… and I’m not sure what my parents would’ve said. I was active enough, I guess, but I can’t say I was running or climbing all the time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Tickets, receipts, notes, customer jobs—they all end up piled together, and then I don’t know what to handle first.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on a single task at work?\n\nPatient: Yes. I can start checking the day’s dry-cleaning orders, but after a few minutes my mind just… goes somewhere else. Labels, choir things, stuff at home. Orders have gone out late, and I’ve mixed up billing more than once. When I try to sort everything, it just feels like too much. The tickets, receipts, notes, customer jobs… they all end up in one pile, and then I can’t tell what I’m supposed to do first.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble organizing and focusing, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: It’s not really new. I mean… even back in grade school, teachers would tell my mother I was bright, but scattered. I’ll jump in before my daughter or a customer is even done asking, or during a training, and then I realize I answered the wrong thing. I mean… I get irritated sometimes when plans change, like anyone, but I don’t know that I’d call it trouble controlling my emotions. I usually just try to keep it to myself.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often described as physically overactive, such as running, climbing, or having difficulty playing quietly?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I don’t remember being told that much… and I’m not sure what my parents would’ve said. I was active enough, I guess, but I can’t say I was running or climbing all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been unable to work or participate in activities you enjoy because of your difficulties with organization and focus?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’ve still gone to work, but it has caused problems. At the shop there’ve been late orders and mix-ups with billing, and that’s led to some tense moments with customers and the other staff.\n\nAnd at church, I’ve kind of stepped back from helping lead planning things, because people were getting frustrated with me. I don’t like saying that, but… yes, it’s affected things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact on your overall quality of life due to these organizational and focus issues?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has. It’s made things tense with the other staff, and a few customers too.\n\nAt church, I’ve kind of stepped back from helping lead planning things because people were getting frustrated with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you're trying to organize your work or tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. When I try to sort everything, it just feels like too much. The tickets, receipts, notes, customer jobs… they all end up in one pile, and then I can’t tell what I’m supposed to do first.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble organizing and focusing, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: It’s not really new. I mean… even back in grade school, teachers would tell my mother I was bright, but scattered. I’d talk out of turn, lose papers, and need reminders to finish my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, like you can't sit still?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess so. In meetings, like at church or those fabric-care trainings, I’ll keep finding reasons to get up—water, papers, checking on something. Everybody else is sitting, and I just… don’t stay put very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do that sometimes. I’ll jump in before my daughter or a customer is even done asking, or during a training, and then I realize I answered the wrong thing. It’s embarrassing, so I try not to, but… it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble controlling your emotions when things don't go as planned?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… I get irritated sometimes when plans change, like anyone, but I don’t know that I’d call it trouble controlling my emotions. I usually just try to keep it to myself.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often described as physically overactive, such as running, climbing, or having difficulty playing quietly?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I don’t remember being told that much… and I’m not sure what my parents would’ve said. I was active enough, I guess, but I can’t say I was running or climbing all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been unable to work or participate in activities you enjoy because of your difficulties with organization and focus?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’ve still gone to work, but it has caused problems. At the shop there’ve been late orders and mix-ups with billing, and that’s led to some tense moments with customers and the other staff.\n\nAnd at church, I’ve kind of stepped back from helping lead planning things, because people were getting frustrated with me. I don’t like saying that, but… yes, it’s affected things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact on your overall quality of life due to these organizational and focus issues?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has. Mostly with work and church. At the shop, the late orders and billing mix-ups have made things tense with customers and coworkers. And at church, I’ve stepped back from leading some planning because people were getting frustrated with me. It’s… not a great feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with organization and focus consistently throughout your adult life as well?\n\nPatient: Not really consistently my whole adult life, no. It’s been more of a real problem these last couple years… about two years or so, since I started changing the business into that more complicated boutique kind of setup. That’s when it started causing actual trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty with impulsivity, such as acting without much thought or consideration for the consequences?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. I’ll jump in with an answer before someone’s even finished asking—my daughter, a customer, even the trainer—and then I realize I answered the wrong thing. It’s embarrassing, so I try not to, but it happens.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s made things tense with the other staff, and a few customers too.\n\nAt church, I’ve kind of stepped back from helping lead planning things because people were getting frustrated with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you're trying to organize your work or tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. When I try to sort everything, it just feels like too much. The tickets, receipts, notes, customer jobs… they all end up in one pile, and then I can’t tell what I’m supposed to do first.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble organizing and focusing, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: It’s not really new. I mean… even back in grade school, teachers would tell my mother I was bright, but scattered. I’d talk out of turn, lose papers, and need reminders to finish my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, like you can't sit still?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess so. In meetings, like at church or those fabric-care trainings, I’ll keep finding reasons to get up—water, papers, checking on something. Everybody else is sitting, and I just… don’t stay put very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do that sometimes. I’ll jump in before my daughter or a customer is even done asking, or during a training, and then I realize I answered the wrong thing. It’s embarrassing, so I try not to, but… it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble controlling your emotions when things don't go as planned?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… I get irritated sometimes when plans change, like anyone, but I don’t know that I’d call it trouble controlling my emotions. I usually just try to keep it to myself.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often described as physically overactive, such as running, climbing, or having difficulty playing quietly?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I don’t remember being told that much… and I’m not sure what my parents would’ve said. I was active enough, I guess, but I can’t say I was running or climbing all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been unable to work or participate in activities you enjoy because of your difficulties with organization and focus?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’ve still gone to work, but it has caused problems. At the shop there’ve been late orders and mix-ups with billing, and that’s led to some tense moments with customers and the other staff.\n\nAnd at church, I’ve kind of stepped back from helping lead planning things, because people were getting frustrated with me. I don’t like saying that, but… yes, it’s affected things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact on your overall quality of life due to these organizational and focus issues?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has. Mostly with work and church. At the shop, the late orders and billing mix-ups have made things tense with customers and coworkers. And at church, I’ve stepped back from leading some planning because people were getting frustrated with me. It’s… not a great feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with organization and focus consistently throughout your adult life as well?\n\nPatient: Not really consistently my whole adult life, no. It’s been more of a real problem these last couple years… about two years or so, since I started changing the business into that more complicated boutique kind of setup. That’s when it started causing actual trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty with impulsivity, such as acting without much thought or consideration for the consequences?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. I’ll jump in with an answer before someone’s even finished asking—my daughter, a customer, even the trainer—and then I realize I answered the wrong thing. It’s embarrassing, so I try not to, but it happens.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life beyond what you've already mentioned?\n\nPatient: I guess… not much beyond that. It’s mostly the shop and church stuff. The late orders, billing getting mixed up, people getting tense with me… and at church I’ve backed off planning things because folks were getting frustrated. That’s been the main problem.\n\nDoctor: Were you often inattentive or easily distracted as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. In grade school, teachers would tell my mother I was bright but kind of scattered. I talked out of turn, lost papers… needed reminding to finish my work. Stuff like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with organization and focus at home, not just at work or church?\n\nPatient: Mostly at work, honestly. That’s what brought me in. I just can’t seem to keep things in order anymore—tickets, receipts, notes, customer jobs… they all end up piled together, and then I don’t know what to handle first.\n\nDoctor: Were you often described as being overly active or restless as a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t remember being called “overly active” exactly… maybe a little fidgety, but I don’t know.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

I’ll jump in before my daughter or a customer is even done asking, or during a training, and then I realize I answered the wrong thing. It’s embarrassing, so I try not to, but… it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble controlling your emotions when things don't go as planned?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… I get irritated sometimes when plans change, like anyone, but I don’t know that I’d call it trouble controlling my emotions. I usually just try to keep it to myself.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often described as physically overactive, such as running, climbing, or having difficulty playing quietly?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I don’t remember being told that much… and I’m not sure what my parents would’ve said. I was active enough, I guess, but I can’t say I was running or climbing all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been unable to work or participate in activities you enjoy because of your difficulties with organization and focus?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’ve still gone to work, but it has caused problems. At the shop there’ve been late orders and mix-ups with billing, and that’s led to some tense moments with customers and the other staff.\n\nAnd at church, I’ve kind of stepped back from helping lead planning things, because people were getting frustrated with me. I don’t like saying that, but… yes, it’s affected things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact on your overall quality of life due to these organizational and focus issues?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has. Mostly with work and church. At the shop, the late orders and billing mix-ups have made things tense with customers and coworkers. And at church, I’ve stepped back from leading some planning because people were getting frustrated with me. It’s… not a great feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with organization and focus consistently throughout your adult life as well?\n\nPatient: Not really consistently my whole adult life, no. It’s been more of a real problem these last couple years… about two years or so, since I started changing the business into that more complicated boutique kind of setup. That’s when it started causing actual trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty with impulsivity, such as acting without much thought or consideration for the consequences?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. I’ll jump in with an answer before someone’s even finished asking—my daughter, a customer, even the trainer—and then I realize I answered the wrong thing. It’s embarrassing, so I try not to, but it happens.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life beyond what you've already mentioned?\n\nPatient: I guess… not much beyond that. It’s mostly the shop and church stuff. The late orders, billing getting mixed up, people getting tense with me… and at church I’ve backed off planning things because folks were getting frustrated. That’s been the main problem.\n\nDoctor: Were you often inattentive or easily distracted as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. In grade school, teachers would tell my mother I was bright but kind of scattered. I talked out of turn, lost papers… needed reminding to finish my work. Stuff like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with organization and focus at home, not just at work or church?\n\nPatient: Mostly at work, honestly. That’s what brought me in. I just can’t seem to keep things in order anymore—tickets, receipts, notes, customer jobs… they all end up piled together, and then I don’t know what to handle first.\n\nDoctor: Were you often described as being overly active or restless as a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t remember being called “overly active” exactly… maybe a little fidgety, but I don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Were your difficulties with organization and focus a problem for you as a child, or did they develop later in life?\n\nPatient: I think it was there when I was a kid, yeah. In grade school, teachers told my mother I was bright, but scattered… I’d talk out of turn, lose papers, and needed reminding to finish my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety at home, outside of work or church meetings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. At home I’m usually just tired… I might shift around some, but I haven’t really paid attention to it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

It’s… not a great feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with organization and focus consistently throughout your adult life as well?\n\nPatient: Not really consistently my whole adult life, no. It’s been more of a real problem these last couple years… about two years or so, since I started changing the business into that more complicated boutique kind of setup. That’s when it started causing actual trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty with impulsivity, such as acting without much thought or consideration for the consequences?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. I’ll jump in with an answer before someone’s even finished asking—my daughter, a customer, even the trainer—and then I realize I answered the wrong thing. It’s embarrassing, so I try not to, but it happens.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life beyond what you've already mentioned?\n\nPatient: I guess… not much beyond that. It’s mostly the shop and church stuff. The late orders, billing getting mixed up, people getting tense with me… and at church I’ve backed off planning things because folks were getting frustrated. That’s been the main problem.\n\nDoctor: Were you often inattentive or easily distracted as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. In grade school, teachers would tell my mother I was bright but kind of scattered. I talked out of turn, lost papers… needed reminding to finish my work. Stuff like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with organization and focus at home, not just at work or church?\n\nPatient: Mostly at work, honestly. That’s what brought me in. I just can’t seem to keep things in order anymore—tickets, receipts, notes, customer jobs… they all end up piled together, and then I don’t know what to handle first.\n\nDoctor: Were you often described as being overly active or restless as a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t remember being called “overly active” exactly… maybe a little fidgety, but I don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Were your difficulties with organization and focus a problem for you as a child, or did they develop later in life?\n\nPatient: I think it was there when I was a kid, yeah. In grade school, teachers told my mother I was bright, but scattered… I’d talk out of turn, lose papers, and needed reminding to finish my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety at home, outside of work or church meetings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. At home I’m usually just tired… I might shift around some, but I haven’t really paid attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced problems in your personal relationships, such as with your family or friends, due to your difficulties with organization and focus?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I don’t like to make a big thing of it, but at the shop there’ve been late orders and mix-ups with billing, and that’s caused some tense moments with staff and customers. And at church, I’ve stepped back from helping lead some planning because people were getting frustrated with me."
